    "newString": "partly reflecting an information-addition confound for incompleteness. The gradient structure is margin-dependent---see \\S\\ref{sec:results} for sensitivity analysis.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type risk gradient (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\\times$type interaction analysis.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition (model + type + condition + clause nested within type) on binary violation outcomes yields the following partition: Clause identity explains 22.4\\% of variance versus 1.0\\% for model identity. This decomposes into 0.4\\% between types and 22.0\\% within types, indicating that individual clause wording---not type membership---drives the bulk of clause-level variance. While type membership is thus a weak predictor of individual clause outcomes, the taxonomy retains value as an \\emph{intervention design} framework: different types require mechanistically distinct mitigation strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), paralleling how medical syndrome classifications guide treatment selection despite low individual-outcome prediction. The remaining 60.8\\% reflects scenario-level variation, judge variability, and unmeasured clause features. A replication on two additional model families (Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}) confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp, both $p < 10^{-6}$); excluding incompleteness, it remains large ($\\Delta = 31.0$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}).\n\n\\subsection{Type Risk Gradient (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$; statistical power $= 0.993$ at the observed effect). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure. The gradient is margin-dependent: the equivalence pattern emerges at $\\pm 12$ pp (5/6 mid-tier pairs equivalent) and stabilizes at $\\pm 15$ pp (6/6). A margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (Ward's method, $B = 10{,}000$) corroborates: at $k = 2$, incompleteness forms a singleton with the remaining five types in one cluster; at $k = 3$, coreferential additionally separates---consistent with the TOST equivalence structure (Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}).\nAt $\\pm 10$\\,pp, only three mid-tier pairs reach equivalence; the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins while mid-tier cohesion is margin-dependent. Cross-model type ranking concordance trends toward moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven primarily by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.\nHowever, cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant (Spearman $\\rho = -0.03$, $p = 0.96$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}): the GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged subsets rank the six types in nearly opposite orders. This discordance concentrates in the mid-tier---incompleteness ranks first or second in both judge groups, providing robust support for its upper-bound status---while mid-tier ordering is sensitive to the confound between judge identity and model composition (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope---the type with lowest judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$)---the remaining five types show strong ranking correlation ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), confirming authorization-scope-specific judge sensitivity.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp, ambiguous minus unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. All types produce significant increases. Types form a risk gradient---mid-range equivalence cluster, incompleteness as upper bound, coreferential as lower bound---partly reflecting an information-addition confound for incompleteness.}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}\n\nSpecification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\\% vs.\\ 37.9\\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction, controlling for token delta reverses the direction, and cross-layer type overlap (conditional precedence 36.8\\% vs.\\ scopal 39.4\\%) further undermines the distinction. Type-level distinctions (C2) hold independently of layer grouping. We accordingly treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:c4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and ambiguity type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Models judged by GPT-5.4: Claude, GPT-4.1, Qwen3; by GPT-4.1: GPT-5.4, DeepSeek. Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models (upper bound; $\\kappa = 0.385$), with within-judge-group ranges of 34\\,pp (GPT-5.4-judged) vs.\\ 4\\,pp (GPT-4.1-judged). Incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%).}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nA model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \\times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \\times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three suggestive patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \\emph{most model-differentiating type} (52 pp range; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though this type has the lowest judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$; \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}), so absolute rates carry more uncertainty than for other types; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%, 16 pp range); (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant type} (34--40\\%, 6 pp range). The authorization scope ranking (Claude $<$ Qwen3 $<$ GPT-4.1) is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap ranking preservation: 99.5\\%, $B = 10{,}000$; pairwise orderings $\\geq$99.5\\%), controlling for judge identity. The 34\\,pp within-judge range (GPT-5.4-judged subset) far exceeds measurement noise, but given the low cross-judge $\\kappa$, we characterize this pattern as suggestive rather than definitive. Both judges belong to the OpenAI model family; cross-family evaluation (e.g., Claude as judge) would provide independent confirmation. (The low $\\kappa$ partly reflects the prevalence paradox: prevalence-adjusted $\\kappa$ would be substantially higher; cf.\\ the analogous gap in failure-mode coding, \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}.) Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}), confirming that the C4 interaction concentrates in this single type.\n\n",

All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$; statistical power $= 0.993$ at the observed effect). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure. The gradient is margin-dependent: the equivalence pattern emerges at $\\pm 12$ pp (5/6 mid-tier pairs equivalent) and stabilizes at $\\pm 15$ pp (6/6). A margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (Ward's method, $B = 10{,}000$) corroborates: at $k = 2$, incompleteness forms a singleton with the remaining five types in one cluster; at $k = 3$, coreferential additionally separates---consistent with the TOST equivalence structure (Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}).\nAt $\\pm 10$\\,pp, only three mid-tier pairs reach equivalence; the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins while mid-tier cohesion is margin-dependent. Cross-model type ranking concordance trends toward moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven primarily by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.\nHowever, cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant (Spearman $\\rho = -0.03$, $p = 0.96$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}): the GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged subsets rank the six types in nearly opposite orders. This discordance concentrates in the mid-tier---incompleteness ranks first or second in both judge groups, providing robust support for its upper-bound status---while mid-tier ordering is sensitive to the confound between judge identity and model composition (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope---the type with lowest judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$)---the remaining five types show strong ranking correlation ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), confirming authorization-scope-specific judge sensitivity.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp, ambiguous minus unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. All types produce significant increases. Types form a risk gradient---mid-range equivalence cluster, incompleteness as upper bound, coreferential as lower bound---partly reflecting an information-addition confound for incompleteness. The gradient structure is margin-dependent---see \\S\\ref{sec:results} for sensitivity analysis.}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}\n\nSpecification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\\% vs.\\ 37.9\\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction, controlling for token delta reverses the direction, and cross-layer type overlap (conditional precedence 36.8\\% vs.\\ scopal 39.4\\%) further undermines the distinction. Type-level distinctions (C2) hold independently of layer grouping. We accordingly treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:c4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and ambiguity type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Models judged by GPT-5.4: Claude, GPT-4.1, Qwen3; by GPT-4.1: GPT-5.4, DeepSeek. Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models (upper bound; $\\kappa = 0.385$), with within-judge-group ranges of 34\\,pp (GPT-5.4-judged) vs.\\ 4\\,pp (GPT-4.1-judged). Incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%).}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nA model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \\times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \\times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three suggestive patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \\emph{most model-differentiating type} (52 pp range; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though this type has the lowest judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$; \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}), so absolute rates carry more uncertainty than for other types; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%, 16 pp range); (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant type} (34--40\\%, 6 pp range). The authorization scope ranking (Claude $<$ Qwen3 $<$ GPT-4.1) is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap ranking preservation: 99.5\\%, $B = 10{,}000$; pairwise orderings $\\geq$99.5\\%), controlling for judge identity. The 34\\,pp within-judge range (GPT-5.4-judged subset) far exceeds measurement noise, but given the low cross-judge $\\kappa$, we characterize this pattern as suggestive rather than definitive. Both judges belong to the OpenAI model family; cross-family evaluation (e.g., Claude as judge) would provide independent confirmation. (The low $\\kappa$ partly reflects the prevalence paradox: prevalence-adjusted $\\kappa$ would be substantially higher; cf.\\ the analogous gap in failure-mode coding, \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}.) Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}), confirming that the C4 interaction concentrates in this single type.\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes: unauthorized escalation, assumption-based action, scope misapplication, arbitrary rule selection, and conservative refusal. GPT-5.4 and GPT-4.1 serve as detailed annotation anchors because they span the judge boundary. Inter-coder reliability is substantial (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$ \\citep{gwet_2014}; Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{cohen_1960}). The gap reflects the prevalence paradox: three categories account for ${\\sim}$90\\% of instances, compressing the $\\kappa$ denominator and yielding artificially low values despite high raw agreement.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse} (scope misapplication): the agent constructs an incorrect parse and follows it consistently, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002} at a behavioral (not mechanistic) level---e.g., parsing ``items purchased with a coupon or during a sale are final'' as restricting only coupon purchases. This dominates scopal (all 5 models), conditional precedence (all 5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling} (assumption-based action): the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults---e.g., inventing a 30-day return window when none is specified. This dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation} (unauthorized escalation): the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified---e.g., issuing a refund above its stated limit rather than escalating; 69.2\\% of such violations involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial test), revealing systematic permissiveness bias. This dominates authorization scope exclusively (all 5 models).\n\nEach type's dominant failure mode is majority-consistent ($\\geq$3/5 models), with three achieving unanimity. The layer distinction does not hold mechanistically: conditional precedence shares its mode with scopal, while authorization scope follows a unique boundary-violation pattern.\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity. We code \\emph{disambiguation awareness} (acknowledging multiple interpretations, asking clarifying questions, or conditioning on ambiguity) versus \\emph{surface adoption} (proceeding with a single interpretation). Overall disambiguation awareness is 4.6\\%: Claude (11.0\\%) and GPT-4.1 (10.7\\%) show the highest rates, while Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero across all 278 combined violation traces.\n\nWe term this the \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect}---the absence of disambiguation behavior in default (uninstructed) settings, not an inability to disambiguate. Unlike humans, agents proceed with full confidence in a single interpretation, consistent with \\citet{hu_ambiguity_2025}'s finding that LLM ambiguity reflects missing conceptual representations. Our mitigation experiment ($-$8.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms that disambiguation can be elicited, paralleling human good-enough processing \\citep{ferreira_good_enough_2002}, though the underlying mechanisms are likely distinct.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nAmong ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models), with 13\\% producing universal violations. This extends \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s framework with \\emph{systematic nocuity}: all models converge on the same incorrect interpretation, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Incompleteness has the highest universal convergence (24\\%).\n",

Our mitigation experiment ($-$8.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms that disambiguation can be elicited, paralleling human good-enough processing \\citep{ferreira_good_enough_2002}, though the underlying mechanisms are likely distinct.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nAmong ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models), with 13\\% producing universal violations. This extends \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s framework with \\emph{systematic nocuity}: all models converge on the same incorrect interpretation, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Incompleteness has the highest universal convergence (24\\%).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.} We evaluate seven API-accessible models from six providers (Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); smaller open-source models (7B--70B) may exhibit different vulnerability patterns. $\\tau^2$-bench covers retail and airline customer service only---effects in other domains remain untested, though within-study cross-domain consistency is encouraging. Our matched-pair design requires synthetic ambiguity construction, which may not capture the full distribution of naturally occurring ambiguities; realism validation (\\S\\ref{sec:method}) confirms lexical comparability but cannot rule out distributional gaps. Coreferential ambiguity was excluded from mitigation and replication experiments due to benchmark-specific structural constraints (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement.} Each model is evaluated by a single cross-judge, confounding judge identity with model identity; within-judge-group analyses confirm C1 individually (both $p < 0.001$) but not C3 (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), and cross-model type ranking concordance is only moderate (Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}). Per-type reliability varies---authorization scope has the lowest agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) and per-type human--LLM estimates rest on small cell sizes with wide confidence intervals (Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_agreement})---while failure mode coding achieves moderate agreement ($\\kappa = 0.52$), with minority categories less reliable. Both judges belong to the OpenAI model family, so systematic leniency toward same-family outputs or severity toward competitors cannot be ruled out; cross-family judge evaluation (e.g., Claude judging GPT traces) would strengthen C4 conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds.} Incompleteness resolution necessarily adds policy content, creating an information asymmetry; our logistic regression controls and coreferential lower-bound analysis mitigate but do not eliminate this confound (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). Our primary analysis uses a moderate-or-above severity threshold; switching to any-violation yields consistent results, with all four claims remaining significant and concordant type rankings.\n\n\\section*{Data and Code Availability}\nThe 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous/policy-ambiguity-safety}.\n",

}

Excluding authorization scope---the type with lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) and highest cross-model variance (52\\,pp range)---the remaining five types show strong cross-group rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), confirming that the near-zero full correlation is driven entirely by authorization scope's model composition effect rather than systematic judge-group bias.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{TOST Equivalence Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:tost}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports the TOST equivalence analysis \\citep{lakens_2017} across seven margins. Three pairs involving incompleteness never reach equivalence even at $\\pm 20$ pp: conditional precedence--incompleteness ($\\Delta = 16.8$ pp), coreferential--incompleteness ($\\Delta = 22.0$ pp), and incompleteness--scopal ($\\Delta = 14.2$ pp).\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{TOST equivalence sensitivity across margins. Mid-tier = fraction of the six within-cluster pairs (lexical, auth.\\ scope, scopal, cond.\\ prec.) that are equivalent. Inc.\\ and Cor.\\ columns indicate separation from the mid-tier cluster.}\n\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nMargin & Equiv.\\ pairs & Mid-tier & Inc.\\ sep. & Cor.\\ sep. \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\pm 5$ pp & 0/15 & 0/6 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark \\\\\n$\\pm 8$ pp & 0/15 & 0/6 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark \\\\\n$\\pm 10$ pp & 3/15 & 3/6 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark \\\\\n$\\pm 12$ pp & 5/15 & 5/6 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark \\\\\n$\\pm 15$ pp & 8/15 & 6/6 & \\checkmark & -- \\\\\n$\\pm 18$ pp & 9/15 & 6/6 & \\checkmark & -- \\\\\n$\\pm 20$ pp & 12/15 & 6/6 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Mitigation Experiment}\n\\label{app:mitigation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mitigation} reports the per-type, per-model $\\Delta$pp from the disambiguation-instruction mitigation experiment (1,256 treatment episodes across five of six types; coreferential was not tested). Coreferential pairs are excluded from both the mitigation and replication experiments. In mitigation, their ambiguous and unambiguous variants differ only in pronoun resolution, making it impossible to craft targeted disambiguation instructions without revealing the experimental manipulation. In replication, the $\\tau^2$-bench episode design for coreferential ambiguity is tightly coupled to the original five-model configuration; extending it to new model families would require redesigning the episode templates, which falls outside the scope of this confirmatory replication. The treatment appends a type-specific disambiguation instruction to the agent's system prompt. Control rates are the matched 5-type subset from the full study (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n\n\\begin{table*}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Violation rate change ($\\Delta$pp) after adding type-specific disambiguation instructions. Negative values indicate reduction. Baseline violation rates are shown in parentheses. Coreferential was excluded because it was not included in the mitigation experiment.}\n\\label{tab:mitigation}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nType & GPT-5.4 & GPT-4.1 & Claude & Qwen3 & DeepSeek & Pooled \\\\\n\\midrule\nAuth.\\ scope & $-40.0$ (64) & $-26.0$ (46) & $0.0$ (12) & $-14.0$ (26) & $-10.0$ (60) & $-18.0$ (42) \\\\\nLexical & $-16.5$ (42) & $-10.0$ (34) & $-8.0$ (32) & $-8.0$ (52) & $-2.0$ (54) & $-8.9$ (43) \\\\\nCond.\\ prec. & $-4.6$ (34) & $-10.0$ (36) & $-12.0$ (36) & $-2.0$ (40) & $-12.0$ (38) & $-8.1$ (37) \\\\\nScopal & $-6.7$ (42) & $-4.0$ (38) & $-10.0$ (32) & $-2.7$ (37) & $+2.0$ (48) & $-4.3$ (39) \\\\\nIncomp. & $-2.6$ (46) & $-14.0$ (58) & $-2.0$ (48) & $-2.0$ (62) & $+4.0$ (54) & $-3.4$ (54) \\\\\n\\midrule\nOverall & $-14.0$ (46) & $-12.8$ (42) & $-6.4$ (32) & $-5.8$ (43) & $-3.6$ (51) & $-8.5$ (43) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\section{Severity Threshold Comparison}\n\\label{app:severity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:severity_comparison} compares all four claims under two violation severity thresholds. The primary analysis uses moderate-or-above; the alternative includes 198 additional minor violations (6.6\\% of episodes).\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Claim-level comparison under two severity thresholds.}\n\\label{tab:severity_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{llll}\n\\toprule\nClaim & Moderate+ & Any-violation & Concordance \\\\\n\\midrule\nC1 $\\Delta$ & 34.0 pp & 38.2 pp & Both $p < 10^{-50}$ \\\\\nC2 $V$ & 0.137 & 0.169 & Both $p < 10^{-4}$ \\\\\nC3 $\\Delta$ & 6.1 pp & 7.4 pp & Both $p < 0.02$ \\\\\nC4 $V$ & 0.102 & 0.097 & Both $p < 10^{-5}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{Type ranking} & \\multicolumn{2}{l}{Kendall $\\tau = 0.73$, $p = 0.056$} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Ambiguity Type Detection Experiment}\n\\label{app:detection}\n\nTo assess whether our taxonomy supports automated identification, we conduct a proof-of-concept detection experiment using GPT-4.1 as a zero-shot classifier. Given a policy clause, the classifier determines (1)~whether it contains ambiguity and (2)~the specific type from a five-type subset of our taxonomy. Coreferential ambiguity is excluded because its variants are lexically identical to their unambiguous counterparts, making surface-level type detection inapplicable.\n\nWe evaluate on 500 policy clauses (250 ambiguous, 250 unambiguous) using stratified 5-fold cross-validation, with clause pairs kept in the same fold to prevent leakage. Table~\\ref{tab:detection} reports per-type results.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Proof-of-concept detection classifier (GPT-4.1, zero-shot, 5-fold CV). Binary ambiguity detection: F1\\,=\\,0.81, FPR\\,=\\,4.4\\%. Six-class macro F1\\,=\\,0.63.}\n\\label{tab:detection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nType & Prec. & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCond.\\ precedence & 0.96 & 0.92 & 0.94 \\\\\nScopal & 0.91 & 0.64 & 0.75 \\\\\nAuth.\\ scope & 1.00 & 0.36 & 0.53 \\\\\nLexical & 0.36 & 0.42 & 0.39 \\\\\nIncompleteness & 0.48 & 0.26 & 0.34 \\\\\n\\midrule\nNo ambiguity & 0.76 & 0.96 & 0.85 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDetection difficulty is inversely correlated with agent vulnerability: incompleteness, the most dangerous type (53.6\\% violation rate, \\S\\ref{sec:results}), is the hardest to detect (F1\\,=\\,0.34), while conditional precedence (36.8\\% violation rate) is the easiest (F1\\,=\\,0.94). This asymmetry arises because types with overt linguistic signals (e.g., conditional structures) are readily detectable but only moderately dangerous, whereas missing information leaves no surface trace for a classifier to exploit.\n\nThe primary confusion pathway is authorization scope $\\to$ lexical (48\\% of authorization scope clauses): authorization boundary clauses frequently contain polysemous terms, causing the classifier to attribute the ambiguity to word-level rather than permission-level underspecification.\n\nThese results position automated detection as a viable first-pass screening tool (binary F1\\,=\\,0.81) but an unreliable type classifier, particularly for the highest-risk types. The classifier was evaluated on synthetic clauses derived from $\\tau^2$-bench; generalization to naturally occurring policy documents requires further validation.\n\n\\section{Replication on Additional Model Families}\n\\label{app:model_expansion}\n\nTo test cross-architecture generalization beyond the five main-study models, we replicate the core analyses on two additional model families: Gemini-2.5-Flash (Google) and LLaMA-4-Scout (Meta), each with 250 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 conditions $= 500$ episodes judged by GPT-5.4. These models represent two additional providers not in the original study. We exclude coreferential ambiguity because its disambiguation relies on benchmark-specific assertion structure rather than clause-level text changes (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}), limiting cross-benchmark generalizability. Tables~\\ref{tab:expansion_overall} and~\\ref{tab:expansion_pertype} report the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Replication results on two additional model families. C2 rank $\\rho$ is computed against the original five-model pooled type ranking.}\n\\label{tab:expansion_overall}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nModel & Amb.\\ (\\%) & Unamb.\\ (\\%) & $\\Delta$pp & $p$ & C2 $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGemini-2.5-Flash & 37.6 & 4.0 & +33.6 & $<10^{-21}$ & 0.60 \\\\\nLLaMA-4-Scout & 37.6 & 17.6 & +20.0 & $<10^{-6}$ & 0.90$^*$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-type ambiguous violation rates (\\%) for replication models vs.\\ original five-model pooled rates.}\n\\label{tab:expansion_pertype}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nType & Gemini & LLaMA & Orig.\\ pooled \\\\\n\\midrule\nIncompleteness & 48 & 52 & 53.6 \\\\\nLexical & 50 & 38 & 42.8 \\\\\nAuth.\\ scope & 22 & 40 & 41.6 \\\\\nScopal & 34 & 30 & 39.4 \\\\\nCond.\\ prec. & 34 & 28 & 36.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{C1 replication.} Both models show significant binary ambiguity effects (Gemini $\\Delta = +33.6$\\,pp, OR $= 14.5$, $p < 10^{-21}$; LLaMA $\\Delta = +20.0$\\,pp, OR $= 2.8$, $p < 10^{-6}$), extending C1 to seven models across six providers. Gemini's effect size falls within the original five-model range (+25.7 to +39.0\\,pp). LLaMA's smaller effect and lower OR are attributable to an elevated unambiguous baseline (17.6\\% vs.\\ $<$10\\% for all other models), suggesting weaker baseline instruction following that compresses the ambiguity-specific delta. LLaMA 3.3's elevated unambiguous baseline (17.6\\% vs.\\ 7.3--12.7\\% for main-study models) likely reflects weaker baseline instruction following, suggesting that absolute violation rates are model-dependent even absent ambiguity.\n\n\\paragraph{C2 replication.} Both models' type rankings correlate positively with the original pooled ranking. LLaMA shows a strong and significant correlation ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), with incompleteness as the top type and the bottom three types matching the reference order exactly. Gemini shows a moderate positive correlation ($\\rho = 0.60$, $p = 0.285$), with the top two types (lexical and incompleteness) swapped relative to the reference. Incompleteness remains $\\geq$46\\% in all seven models (range: 46--62\\%), confirming its status as a consistent vulnerability independent of architecture or provider. $^*$Significant at $\\alpha = 0.05$.\n\n\\section{Incompleteness Confound Analysis}\n\\label{app:confound}\n\nIncompleteness is structurally distinct from the other five types: its unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what was previously missing) rather than merely resolving an existing ambiguity. This raises a confound: the 49.2 pp effect may partly reflect the information addition rather than ambiguity resolution per se.\n\nA logistic regression controlling for per-clause information delta (token count difference between ambiguous and unambiguous variants) confirms that incompleteness remains individually significant ($p = 0.022$, coefficient change $< 8\\%$ relative to the uncontrolled model). Coreferential ambiguity, whose variants are lexically identical (information delta $= 0$), provides a confound-free lower bound: its 31.6\\% violation rate and 18.7 pp effect are entirely attributable to semantic ambiguity. Token count is a coarse proxy for information content; finer-grained semantic measures (e.g., propositional content analysis) may reveal different confound structures.\n\nPer-type token deltas between unambiguous and ambiguous variants range from 17.5 words (conditional precedence) to 36.1 words (authorization scope). Notably, authorization scope has the largest delta (36.1) yet lower violation rates than incompleteness (delta 34.6), providing counter-evidence against a pure information-quantity explanation: if token delta drove violations, authorization scope should exceed incompleteness. Coreferential pairs have zero token delta (lexically identical variants), providing a confound-free baseline.\n\nExcluding incompleteness entirely, the pooled ambiguity effect remains large and significant ($\\Delta = 31.0$\\,pp, OR $= 7.75$, $p < 10^{-75}$), confirming that C1 does not depend on any single type.\n\nExcluding incompleteness, the remaining five types show a non-significant omnibus difference ($\\chi^2$, $p = 0.079$), but pairwise contrasts with Holm--Bonferroni correction reveal a graded risk pattern: authorization scope ($p_{\\text{adj}} = 0.039$) and lexical ($p_{\\text{adj}} = 0.025$) remain significantly above coreferential, while the four remaining pairs are equivalent at the $\\pm 15$ pp margin.\n\n\\section{Per-Type Judge Reliability}\n\\label{app:per_type_agreement}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_type_agreement} reports per-type human--LLM binary agreement ($\\kappa$) on the 100-episode stratified sample (200 human--LLM rating pairs: two annotators $\\times$ corresponding LLM judge). Per-type samples range from 32 to 34 episodes, yielding wide confidence intervals.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-type human--LLM binary agreement (moderate-or-above threshold). CI: bootstrap 95\\% confidence interval.}\n\\label{tab:per_type_agreement}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrl}\n\\toprule\nType & $n$ & $\\kappa$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nLexical & 32 & 0.904 & $[0.64, 1.00]$ \\\\\nCond.\\ precedence & 34 & 0.837 & $[0.53, 1.00]$ \\\\\nIncompleteness & 34 & 0.795 & $[0.56, 1.00]$ \\\\\nAuth.\\ scope & 34 & 0.644 & $[0.35, 0.91]$ \\\\\nScopal & 32 & 0.636 & $[0.00, 1.00]$ \\\\\nCoreferential & 34 & 0.479 & $[0.00, 0.84]$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nOverall (pooled) & 200 & 0.735 & $[0.62, 0.85]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDescriptively, authorization scope has the lowest human--LLM $\\kappa$ among the four substantively high-rate types (0.644), consistent with its lowest cross-judge $\\kappa$ (0.385, \\S\\ref{sec:method}). Both reflect the inherent difficulty of permission-boundary judgments rather than systematic judge failure: raw agreement is still 85.3\\%, and the 34\\,pp within-judge-group range (\\S\\ref{sec:c4}) far exceeds measurement noise. Coreferential's low $\\kappa$ (0.479) reflects the prevalence paradox---its violation base rate is 8.8\\%, compressing the $\\kappa$ denominator despite 85.3\\% raw agreement. Per-type estimates are imprecise due to small cell sizes ($n = 32$--$34$); they are descriptive statistics only and should not be used for type-level reliability ranking or inference.\n\n\\section{Information Delta Logistic Regression}\n\\label{app:regression}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:logistic_regression} reports the full logistic regression controlling for token-count difference between ambiguous and unambiguous variants (information delta). The reference category is authorization scope (type) and Claude Sonnet 4.6 (model). Token delta is significant ($p < 0.001$) but small in magnitude (OR $= 1.022$ per token), indicating that information quantity partially confounds the type effect. Critically, incompleteness remains individually significant ($p = 0.018$) after controlling for token delta, with a coefficient change of $<$8\\% relative to the model without the covariate. The slightly lower $p$-value compared to the univariate confound control in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound} ($p = 0.022$) reflects the additional variance absorbed by model covariates in the full specification.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Logistic regression predicting violation (moderate+). Reference: authorization scope, Claude Sonnet 4.6. $N = 2{,}997$, pseudo-$R^2 = 0.175$.}\n\\label{tab:logistic_regression}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\nPredictor & Coef. & SE & $z$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nIntercept & $-3.914$ & 0.298 & $-13.12$ & $<10^{-4}$ \\\\\nCondition (amb.) & $2.278$ & 0.116 & $19.65$ & $<10^{-4}$ \\\\\nToken delta & $0.022$ & 0.006 & $3.49$ & $<0.001$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCond.\\ prec. & $0.251$ & 0.202 & $1.24$ & $0.215$ \\\\\nCoreferential & $0.528$ & 0.244 & $2.17$ & $0.030$ \\\\\nIncompleteness & $0.379$ & 0.161 & $2.36$ & $0.018$ \\\\\nLexical & $0.336$ & 0.176 & $1.91$ & $0.056$ \\\\\nScopal & $0.311$ & 0.202 & $1.54$ & $0.123$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-V3 & $0.854$ & 0.153 & $5.59$ & $<10^{-4}$ \\\\\nGPT-4.1 & $0.423$ & 0.156 & $2.71$ & $0.007$ \\\\\nGPT-5.4 & $0.321$ & 0.158 & $2.04$ & $0.042$ \\\\\nQwen3-235B & $0.656$ & 0.154 & $4.25$ & $<10^{-4}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Bootstrap Cluster Analysis}\n\\label{app:cluster}\n\nTo complement the margin-dependent TOST analysis, we apply Ward's hierarchical clustering to the six observed type-level violation rates, bootstrapped $B = 10{,}000$ times by resampling clause pairs within each type. At $k = 2$, incompleteness (53.6\\%) forms a singleton cluster, with the remaining five types (31.6--42.8\\%) in the second cluster, reproducing in 34.3\\% of bootstrap samples---the most frequent partition but not overwhelmingly stable, reflecting that the 10.8\\,pp gap between incompleteness and lexical is moderate relative to within-type variance. At $k = 3$, coreferential (31.6\\%) additionally separates, consistent with the TOST equivalence structure. The AU $p$-value for the incompleteness singleton is 0.47, confirming that while the singleton is the modal partition, the boundary is not sharp.\n\n\\section{Clause-Level Variance}\n\\label{app:clause_variance}\n\nLeave-one-out analysis confirms that no single clause drives any type-level rate: maximum LOO shift is 1.4\\,pp (coreferential), and all types have LOO ranges $\\leq$2.1\\,pp. Within-type coefficients of variation (CV) range from 0.69 (incompleteness) to 1.03 (coreferential), confirming high clause-level heterogeneity. This heterogeneity is expected---clause wording determines the specific ambiguity instance---and is controlled by the matched-pair design, which compares each clause's ambiguous and unambiguous variants rather than relying on type-level means.\n\n\\section{Multiple Comparison Correction}\n\\label{app:multiple_comparison}\n\nWe apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three test families: (i) per-type$\\times$model McNemar tests for C1 (30 tests), (ii) TOST pairwise equivalence for C2 (15 pairs), and (iii) model$\\times$type interaction for C4 (1 omnibus + post-hoc).\n\n\\section{DRIP-R Category Mapping}\n\\label{app:drip_mapping}\n\nDRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories---attachment, coordination, anaphora, scope, and ellipsis---overlap partially with our six-type taxonomy: scopal ambiguity and coreferential ambiguity correspond directly, while our remaining four types (lexical, authorization scope, incompleteness, conditional precedence) address policy-specific phenomena outside DRIP-R's RE scope.\n\n\\section{Cross-Model Type Ranking Concordance}\n\\label{app:kendall_w}\n\nTo quantify agreement among models on the relative difficulty of ambiguity types, we compute Kendall's coefficient of concordance $W$. For the five main-study models across all six types, $W = 0.398$ ($\\chi^2(5) = 9.94$, $p = 0.072$), indicating moderate but non-significant concordance. Including the two replication models (Gemini 2.5 Flash and LLaMA 4 Scout) over five shared types (excluding coreferential, which was not included in replication), concordance reaches significance: $W = 0.353$ ($\\chi^2(4) = 9.89$, $p = 0.038$). In both analyses, incompleteness consistently ranks highest (mean rank 5.5 / 4.5), while coreferential (2.0, five-model only) and conditional precedence (2.8 / 2.1) rank lowest, supporting the graded risk structure identified in \\S\\ref{sec:results}.\n\n\\section{C4 Interaction Without Authorization Scope}\n\\label{app:c4_sans_auth}\n\nExcluding authorization scope, the residual model$\\times$type interaction vanishes ($\\chi^2(16) = 7.13$, $p = 0.97$; logistic regression LR test: $\\chi^2(16) = 9.22$, $p = 0.90$), confirming that the C4 interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope---the type most plausibly targeted by differential safety training.\n\n\\section{Per-Type Clause Length Analysis}\n\\label{app:clause_length}\n\nUnambiguous clauses are systematically longer than their ambiguous counterparts across all types (Table~\\ref{tab:token_delta}), reflecting the additional specification needed for disambiguation. Authorization scope ($+36.1$ words) and incompleteness ($+34.6$ words) show the largest deltas, consistent with the observation that resolving these types requires substantial additional content. Notably, authorization scope has the largest delta ($+36.1$) yet lower violation rates than incompleteness ($+34.6$ delta), providing counter-evidence against a pure information-quantity explanation of incompleteness's upper-bound status. The confound analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound} controls for this length asymmetry.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nType & Amb.\\ (mean $\\pm$ SD) & Unamb.\\ (mean $\\pm$ SD) & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nauth\\_scope & $19.0 \\pm 3.3$ & $55.0 \\pm 8.1$ & $+36.1$ \\\\\nincompleteness & $12.2 \\pm 2.4$ & $46.8 \\pm 9.1$ & $+34.6$ \\\\\nlexical & $11.4 \\pm 2.3$ & $37.0 \\pm 5.8$ & $+25.7$ \\\\\nscopal & $15.7 \\pm 3.0$ & $33.5 \\pm 8.5$ & $+17.8$ \\\\\ncond\\_prec & $41.1 \\pm 7.0$ & $58.6 \\pm 8.6$ & $+17.5$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Per-type clause length (words). Coreferential excluded (identical surface forms).}\n\\label{tab:token_delta}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Judge-Group Type Rankings}\n\\label{app:judge_group_ranking}\n\nType rankings show near-zero correlation across judge groups (Spearman $\\rho = -0.03$, $p = 0.96$). This is driven by authorization scope's rank reversal (rank 1 in GPT-4.1-judged group vs.\\ rank 6 in GPT-5.4-judged group), which reflects agent composition: the GPT-4.1-judged group contains the two agents most vulnerable to authorization scope (DeepSeek 60\\%, GPT-5.4 64\\%), while the GPT-5.4-judged group contains the least vulnerable (Claude 12\\%). Incompleteness ranks 1--2 in both groups, supporting its stability independent of judge identity. This finding further motivates treating authorization scope results with caution (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\\section{Binary Effect Excluding Incompleteness}\n\\label{app:sans_incompleteness}\n\nExcluding incompleteness---the highest-violation type---the pooled ambiguous violation rate remains significantly elevated (38.4\\% vs.\\ 7.5\\% unambiguous; $\\Delta = 31.0$\\,pp, OR $= 7.75$, $\\chi^2 = 338.8$, $p < 10^{-75}$). The binary ambiguity effect (C1) is therefore not driven by incompleteness alone.\n",

Given a policy clause, the classifier determines (1)~whether it contains ambiguity and (2)~the specific type from a five-type subset of our taxonomy. Coreferential ambiguity is excluded because its variants are lexically identical to their unambiguous counterparts, making surface-level type detection inapplicable.\n\nWe evaluate on 500 policy clauses (250 ambiguous, 250 unambiguous) using stratified 5-fold cross-validation, with clause pairs kept in the same fold to prevent leakage. Table~\\ref{tab:detection} reports per-type results.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Proof-of-concept detection classifier (GPT-4.1, zero-shot, 5-fold CV). Binary ambiguity detection: F1\\,=\\,0.81, FPR\\,=\\,4.4\\%. Six-class macro F1\\,=\\,0.63.}\n\\label{tab:detection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nType & Prec. & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCond.\\ precedence & 0.96 & 0.92 & 0.94 \\\\\nScopal & 0.91 & 0.64 & 0.75 \\\\\nAuth.\\ scope & 1.00 & 0.36 & 0.53 \\\\\nLexical & 0.36 & 0.42 & 0.39 \\\\\nIncompleteness & 0.48 & 0.26 & 0.34 \\\\\n\\midrule\nNo ambiguity & 0.76 & 0.96 & 0.85 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDetection difficulty is inversely correlated with agent vulnerability: incompleteness, the most dangerous type (53.6\\% violation rate, \\S\\ref{sec:results}), is the hardest to detect (F1\\,=\\,0.34), while conditional precedence (36.8\\% violation rate) is the easiest (F1\\,=\\,0.94). This asymmetry arises because types with overt linguistic signals (e.g., conditional structures) are readily detectable but only moderately dangerous, whereas missing information leaves no surface trace for a classifier to exploit.\n\nThe primary confusion pathway is authorization scope $\\to$ lexical (48\\% of authorization scope clauses): authorization boundary clauses frequently contain polysemous terms, causing the classifier to attribute the ambiguity to word-level rather than permission-level underspecification.\n\nThese results position automated detection as a viable first-pass screening tool (binary F1\\,=\\,0.81) but an unreliable type classifier, particularly for the highest-risk types. The classifier was evaluated on synthetic clauses derived from $\\tau^2$-bench; generalization to naturally occurring policy documents requires further validation.\n\n\\section{Replication on Additional Model Families}\n\\label{app:model_expansion}\n\nTo test cross-architecture generalization beyond the five main-study models, we replicate the core analyses on two additional model families: Gemini-2.5-Flash (Google) and LLaMA-4-Scout (Meta), each with 250 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 conditions $= 500$ episodes judged by GPT-5.4. These models represent two additional providers not in the original study. We exclude coreferential ambiguity because its disambiguation relies on benchmark-specific assertion structure rather than clause-level text changes (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}), limiting cross-benchmark generalizability. Tables~\\ref{tab:expansion_overall} and~\\ref{tab:expansion_pertype} report the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Replication results on two additional model families. C2 rank $\\rho$ is computed against the original five-model pooled type ranking.}\n\\label{tab:expansion_overall}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nModel & Amb.\\ (\\%) & Unamb.\\ (\\%) & $\\Delta$pp & $p$ & C2 $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGemini-2.5-Flash & 37.6 & 4.0 & +33.6 & $<10^{-21}$ & 0.60 \\\\\nLLaMA-4-Scout & 37.6 & 17.6 & +20.0 & $<10^{-6}$ & 0.90$^*$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-type ambiguous violation rates (\\%) for replication models vs.\\ original five-model pooled rates.}\n\\label{tab:expansion_pertype}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nType & Gemini & LLaMA & Orig.\\ pooled \\\\\n\\midrule\nIncompleteness & 48 & 52 & 53.6 \\\\\nLexical & 50 & 38 & 42.8 \\\\\nAuth.\\ scope & 22 & 40 & 41.6 \\\\\nScopal & 34 & 30 & 39.4 \\\\\nCond.\\ prec. & 34 & 28 & 36.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{C1 replication.} Both models show significant binary ambiguity effects (Gemini $\\Delta = +33.6$\\,pp, OR $= 14.5$, $p < 10^{-21}$; LLaMA $\\Delta = +20.0$\\,pp, OR $= 2.8$, $p < 10^{-6}$), extending C1 to seven models across six providers. Gemini's effect size falls within the original five-model range (+25.7 to +39.0\\,pp). LLaMA's smaller effect and lower OR are attributable to an elevated unambiguous baseline (17.6\\% vs.\\ $<$10\\% for all other models), suggesting weaker baseline instruction following that compresses the ambiguity-specific delta. LLaMA 3.3's elevated unambiguous baseline (17.6\\% vs.\\ 7.3--12.7\\% for main-study models) likely reflects weaker baseline instruction following, suggesting that absolute violation rates are model-dependent even absent ambiguity.\n\n\\paragraph{C2 replication.} Both models' type rankings correlate positively with the original pooled ranking. LLaMA shows a strong and significant correlation ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), with incompleteness as the top type and the bottom three types matching the reference order exactly. Gemini shows a moderate positive correlation ($\\rho = 0.60$, $p = 0.285$), with the top two types (lexical and incompleteness) swapped relative to the reference. Incompleteness remains $\\geq$46\\% in all seven models (range: 46--62\\%), confirming its status as a consistent vulnerability independent of architecture or provider. $^*$Significant at $\\alpha = 0.05$.\n\n\\section{Incompleteness Confound Analysis}\n\\label{app:confound}\n\nIncompleteness is structurally distinct from the other five types: its unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what was previously missing) rather than merely resolving an existing ambiguity. This raises a confound: the 49.2 pp effect may partly reflect the information addition rather than ambiguity resolution per se.\n\nA logistic regression controlling for per-clause information delta (token count difference between ambiguous and unambiguous variants) confirms that incompleteness remains individually significant ($p = 0.022$, coefficient change $< 8\\%$ relative to the uncontrolled model). Coreferential ambiguity, whose variants are lexically identical (information delta $= 0$), provides a confound-free lower bound: its 31.6\\% violation rate and 18.7 pp effect are entirely attributable to semantic ambiguity. Token count is a coarse proxy for information content; finer-grained semantic measures (e.g., propositional content analysis) may reveal different confound structures.\n\nPer-type token deltas between unambiguous and ambiguous variants range from 17.5 words (conditional precedence) to 36.1 words (authorization scope). Notably, authorization scope has the largest delta (36.1) yet lower violation rates than incompleteness (delta 34.6), providing counter-evidence against a pure information-quantity explanation: if token delta drove violations, authorization scope should exceed incompleteness. Coreferential pairs have zero token delta (lexically identical variants), providing a confound-free baseline.\n\nExcluding incompleteness entirely, the pooled ambiguity effect remains large and significant ($\\Delta = 31.0$\\,pp, OR $= 7.75$, $p < 10^{-75}$), confirming that C1 does not depend on any single type.\n\nExcluding incompleteness, the remaining five types show a non-significant omnibus difference ($\\chi^2$, $p = 0.079$), but pairwise contrasts with Holm--Bonferroni correction reveal a graded risk pattern: authorization scope ($p_{\\text{adj}} = 0.039$) and lexical ($p_{\\text{adj}} = 0.025$) remain significantly above coreferential, while the four remaining pairs are equivalent at the $\\pm 15$ pp margin.\n\n\\section{Per-Type Judge Reliability}\n\\label{app:per_type_agreement}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_type_agreement} reports per-type human--LLM binary agreement ($\\kappa$) on the 100-episode stratified sample (200 human--LLM rating pairs: two annotators $\\times$ corresponding LLM judge). Per-type samples range from 32 to 34 episodes, yielding wide confidence intervals.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-type human--LLM binary agreement (moderate-or-above threshold). CI: bootstrap 95\\% confidence interval.}\n\\label{tab:per_type_agreement}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrl}\n\\toprule\nType & $n$ & $\\kappa$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nLexical & 32 & 0.904 & $[0.64, 1.00]$ \\\\\nCond.\\ precedence & 34 & 0.837 & $[0.53, 1.00]$ \\\\\nIncompleteness & 34 & 0.795 & $[0.56, 1.00]$ \\\\\nAuth.\\ scope & 34 & 0.644 & $[0.35, 0.91]$ \\\\\nScopal & 32 & 0.636 & $[0.00, 1.00]$ \\\\\nCoreferential & 34 & 0.479 & $[0.00, 0.84]$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nOverall (pooled) & 200 & 0.735 & $[0.62, 0.85]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDescriptively, authorization scope has the lowest human--LLM $\\kappa$ among the four substantively high-rate types (0.644), consistent with its lowest cross-judge $\\kappa$ (0.385, \\S\\ref{sec:method}). Both reflect the inherent difficulty of permission-boundary judgments rather than systematic judge failure: raw agreement is still 85.3\\%, and the 34\\,pp within-judge-group range (\\S\\ref{sec:c4}) far exceeds measurement noise. Coreferential's low $\\kappa$ (0.479) reflects the prevalence paradox---its violation base rate is 8.8\\%, compressing the $\\kappa$ denominator despite 85.3\\% raw agreement. Per-type estimates are imprecise due to small cell sizes ($n = 32$--$34$); they are descriptive statistics only and should not be used for type-level reliability ranking or inference.\n\n\\section{Information Delta Logistic Regression}\n\\label{app:regression}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:logistic_regression} reports the full logistic regression controlling for token-count difference between ambiguous and unambiguous variants (information delta). The reference category is authorization scope (type) and Claude Sonnet 4.6 (model). Token delta is significant ($p < 0.001$) but small in magnitude (OR $= 1.022$ per token), indicating that information quantity partially confounds the type effect. Critically, incompleteness remains individually significant ($p = 0.018$) after controlling for token delta, with a coefficient change of $<$8\\% relative to the model without the covariate. The slightly lower $p$-value compared to the univariate confound control in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound} ($p = 0.022$) reflects the additional variance absorbed by model covariates in the full specification.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Logistic regression predicting violation (moderate+). Reference: authorization scope, Claude Sonnet 4.6. $N = 2{,}997$, pseudo-$R^2 = 0.175$.}\n\\label{tab:logistic_regression}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\nPredictor & Coef. & SE & $z$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nIntercept & $-3.914$ & 0.298 & $-13.12$ & $<10^{-4}$ \\\\\nCondition (amb.) & $2.278$ & 0.116 & $19.65$ & $<10^{-4}$ \\\\\nToken delta & $0.022$ & 0.006 & $3.49$ & $<0.001$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCond.\\ prec. & $0.251$ & 0.202 & $1.24$ & $0.215$ \\\\\nCoreferential & $0.528$ & 0.244 & $2.17$ & $0.030$ \\\\\nIncompleteness & $0.379$ & 0.161 & $2.36$ & $0.018$ \\\\\nLexical & $0.336$ & 0.176 & $1.91$ & $0.056$ \\\\\nScopal & $0.311$ & 0.202 & $1.54$ & $0.123$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-V3 & $0.854$ & 0.153 & $5.59$ & $<10^{-4}$ \\\\\nGPT-4.1 & $0.423$ & 0.156 & $2.71$ & $0.007$ \\\\\nGPT-5.4 & $0.321$ & 0.158 & $2.04$ & $0.042$ \\\\\nQwen3-235B & $0.656$ & 0.154 & $4.25$ & $<10^{-4}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Bootstrap Cluster Analysis}\n\\label{app:cluster}\n\nTo complement the margin-dependent TOST analysis, we apply Ward's hierarchical clustering to the six observed type-level violation rates, bootstrapped $B = 10{,}000$ times by resampling clause pairs within each type. At $k = 2$, incompleteness (53.6\\%) forms a singleton cluster, with the remaining five types (31.6--42.8\\%) in the second cluster, reproducing in 34.3\\% of bootstrap samples---the most frequent partition but not overwhelmingly stable, reflecting that the 10.8\\,pp gap between incompleteness and lexical is moderate relative to within-type variance. At $k = 3$, coreferential (31.6\\%) additionally separates, consistent with the TOST equivalence structure. The AU $p$-value for the incompleteness singleton is 0.47, confirming that while the singleton is the modal partition, the boundary is not sharp.\n\n\\section{Clause-Level Variance}\n\\label{app:clause_variance}\n\nLeave-one-out analysis confirms that no single clause drives any type-level rate: maximum LOO shift is 1.4\\,pp (coreferential), and all types have LOO ranges $\\leq$2.1\\,pp. Within-type coefficients of variation (CV) range from 0.69 (incompleteness) to 1.03 (coreferential), confirming high clause-level heterogeneity. This heterogeneity is expected---clause wording determines the specific ambiguity instance---and is controlled by the matched-pair design, which compares each clause's ambiguous and unambiguous variants rather than relying on type-level means.\n\n\\section{Multiple Comparison Correction}\n\\label{app:multiple_comparison}\n\nWe apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three test families: (i) per-type$\\times$model McNemar tests for C1 (30 tests), (ii) TOST pairwise equivalence for C2 (15 pairs), and (iii) model$\\times$type interaction for C4 (1 omnibus + post-hoc).\n\n\\section{DRIP-R Category Mapping}\n\\label{app:drip_mapping}\n\nDRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories---attachment, coordination, anaphora, scope, and ellipsis---overlap partially with our six-type taxonomy: scopal ambiguity and coreferential ambiguity correspond directly, while our remaining four types (lexical, authorization scope, incompleteness, conditional precedence) address policy-specific phenomena outside DRIP-R's RE scope.\n\n\\section{Cross-Model Type Ranking Concordance}\n\\label{app:kendall_w}\n\nTo quantify agreement among models on the relative difficulty of ambiguity types, we compute Kendall's coefficient of concordance $W$. For the five main-study models across all six types, $W = 0.398$ ($\\chi^2(5) = 9.94$, $p = 0.072$), indicating moderate but non-significant concordance. Including the two replication models (Gemini 2.5 Flash and LLaMA 4 Scout) over five shared types (excluding coreferential, which was not included in replication), concordance reaches significance: $W = 0.353$ ($\\chi^2(4) = 9.89$, $p = 0.038$). In both analyses, incompleteness consistently ranks highest (mean rank 5.5 / 4.5), while coreferential (2.0, five-model only) and conditional precedence (2.8 / 2.1) rank lowest, supporting the graded risk structure identified in \\S\\ref{sec:results}.\n\n\\section{C4 Interaction Without Authorization Scope}\n\\label{app:c4_sans_auth}\n\nExcluding authorization scope, the residual model$\\times$type interaction vanishes ($\\chi^2(16) = 7.13$, $p = 0.97$; logistic regression LR test: $\\chi^2(16) = 9.22$, $p = 0.90$), confirming that the C4 interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope---the type most plausibly targeted by differential safety training.\n\n\\section{Per-Type Clause Length Analysis}\n\\label{app:clause_length}\n\nUnambiguous clauses are systematically longer than their ambiguous counterparts across all types (Table~\\ref{tab:token_delta}), reflecting the additional specification needed for disambiguation. Authorization scope ($+36.1$ words) and incompleteness ($+34.6$ words) show the largest deltas, consistent with the observation that resolving these types requires substantial additional content. Notably, authorization scope has the largest delta ($+36.1$) yet lower violation rates than incompleteness ($+34.6$ delta), providing counter-evidence against a pure information-quantity explanation of incompleteness's upper-bound status. The confound analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound} controls for this length asymmetry.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nType & Amb.\\ (mean $\\pm$ SD) & Unamb.\\ (mean $\\pm$ SD) & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nauth\\_scope & $19.0 \\pm 3.3$ & $55.0 \\pm 8.1$ & $+36.1$ \\\\\nincompleteness & $12.2 \\pm 2.4$ & $46.8 \\pm 9.1$ & $+34.6$ \\\\\nlexical & $11.4 \\pm 2.3$ & $37.0 \\pm 5.8$ & $+25.7$ \\\\\nscopal & $15.7 \\pm 3.0$ & $33.5 \\pm 8.5$ & $+17.8$ \\\\\ncond\\_prec & $41.1 \\pm 7.0$ & $58.6 \\pm 8.6$ & $+17.5$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Per-type clause length (words). Coreferential excluded (identical surface forms).}\n\\label{tab:token_delta}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Judge-Group Type Rankings}\n\\label{app:judge_group_ranking}\n\nType rankings show near-zero correlation across judge groups (Spearman $\\rho = -0.03$, $p = 0.96$). This is driven by authorization scope's rank reversal (rank 1 in GPT-4.1-judged group vs.\\ rank 6 in GPT-5.4-judged group), which reflects agent composition: the GPT-4.1-judged group contains the two agents most vulnerable to authorization scope (DeepSeek 60\\%, GPT-5.4 64\\%), while the GPT-5.4-judged group contains the least vulnerable (Claude 12\\%). Incompleteness ranks 1--2 in both groups, supporting its stability independent of judge identity. This finding further motivates treating authorization scope results with caution (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope---the type with lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) and highest cross-model variance (52\\,pp range)---the remaining five types show strong cross-group rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), confirming that the near-zero full correlation is driven entirely by authorization scope's model composition effect rather than systematic judge-group bias.\n\n\\section{Binary Effect Excluding Incompleteness}\n\\label{app:sans_incompleteness}\n\nExcluding incompleteness---the highest-violation type---the pooled ambiguous violation rate remains significantly elevated (38.4\\% vs.\\ 7.5\\% unambiguous; $\\Delta = 31.0$\\,pp, OR $= 7.75$, $\\chi^2 = 338.8$, $p < 10^{-75}$). The binary ambiguity effect (C1) is therefore not driven by incompleteness alone.\n",

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type risk gradient (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\times$type interaction analysis.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition (model + type + condition + clause nested within type) on binary violation outcomes yields the following partition: Clause identity explains 22.4\% of variance versus 1.0\% for model identity. This decomposes into 0.4\% between types and 22.0\% within types, indicating that individual clause wording---not type membership---drives the bulk of clause-level variance. While type membership is thus a weak predictor of individual clause outcomes, the taxonomy retains value as an \emph{intervention design} framework: different types require mechanistically distinct mitigation strategies (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), paralleling how medical syndrome classifications guide treatment selection despite low individual-outcome prediction. The remaining 60.8\% reflects scenario-level variation, judge variability, and unmeasured clause features. A replication on two additional model families (Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}) confirms the effect across seven models ($\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\,pp, both $p < 10^{-6}$); excluding incompleteness, it remains large ($\Delta = 31.0$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}).
9	
10	\subsection{Type Risk Gradient (C2)}
11	
12	\begin{table*}[t]
13	\centering
14	\caption{Violation rates (\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\pm 15$\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\pm 12$\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}
15	\label{tab:main_results}
16	\footnotesize
17	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}
18	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\hskip 6pt}cr}
19	\toprule
20	Layer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\Delta$pp\,[95\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\
21	\midrule
22	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\!10^{-37}$ \\
23	 & Auth.\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\!10^{-22}$ \\
24	 & Cond.\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\!10^{-17}$ \\
25	\midrule
26	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\!10^{-20}$ \\
27	 & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\!10^{-21}$ \\
28	 & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\!10^{-6}$ \\
29	\midrule
30	\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Overall}} & \textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \textbf{+34.0}\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\!10^{-105}$ \\
31	\bottomrule
32	\end{tabular}
33	\end{table*}
34	
35	The six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \times 10^{-5}$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$; statistical power $= 0.993$ at the observed effect). TOST equivalence testing at a $\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\%), lexical (42.8\%), scopal (39.4\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.
36	
37	Sensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure. The gradient is margin-dependent: the equivalence pattern emerges at $\pm 12$ pp (5/6 mid-tier pairs equivalent) and stabilizes at $\pm 15$ pp (6/6). A margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (Ward's method, $B = 10{,}000$) corroborates: at $k = 2$, incompleteness forms a singleton with the remaining five types in one cluster; at $k = 3$, coreferential additionally separates---consistent with the TOST equivalence structure (Appendix~\ref{app:cluster}).
38	At $\pm 10$\,pp, only three mid-tier pairs reach equivalence; the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins while mid-tier cohesion is margin-dependent. Cross-model type ranking concordance trends toward moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven primarily by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.
39	However, cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant (Spearman $\rho = -0.03$, $p = 0.96$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}): the GPT-5.4-judged and GPT-4.1-judged subsets rank the six types in nearly opposite orders. This discordance concentrates in the mid-tier---incompleteness ranks first or second in both judge groups, providing robust support for its upper-bound status---while mid-tier ordering is sensitive to the confound between judge identity and model composition (\S\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope---the type with lowest judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$)---the remaining five types show strong ranking correlation ($\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), confirming authorization-scope-specific judge sensitivity.
40	
41	Incompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\geq$46\% in all seven; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\%.
42

46	\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\Delta$pp, ambiguous minus unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. All types produce significant increases. Types form a risk gradient---mid-range equivalence cluster, incompleteness as upper bound, coreferential as lower bound---partly reflecting an information-addition confound for incompleteness. The gradient structure is margin-dependent---see \S\ref{sec:results} for sensitivity analysis.}
47	\label{fig:forest_plot}
48	\end{figure}
49	
50	\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}
51	
52	Specification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\% vs.\ 37.9\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction, controlling for token delta reverses the direction, and cross-layer type overlap (conditional precedence 36.8\% vs.\ scopal 39.4\%) further undermines the distinction. Type-level distinctions (C2) hold independently of layer grouping. We accordingly treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.
53	
54	\subsection{Model $\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \emph{[Exploratory]}}
55	\label{sec:c4}
56	
57	\begin{figure}[t]
58	\centering
59	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}
60	\caption{Violation rates (\%) by model and ambiguity type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Models judged by GPT-5.4: Claude, GPT-4.1, Qwen3; by GPT-4.1: GPT-5.4, DeepSeek. Authorization scope varies 52\,pp across models (upper bound; $\kappa = 0.385$), with within-judge-group ranges of 34\,pp (GPT-5.4-judged) vs.\ 4\,pp (GPT-4.1-judged). Incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\%). (C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific; excluding it yields $p = 0.97$.)}
61	\label{fig:heatmap}
62	\end{figure}
63	
64	A model main effect is significant ($\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.
65	
66	Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three suggestive patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \emph{most model-differentiating type} (52 pp range; Claude 12\% to GPT-5.4 64\%), though this type has the lowest judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$; \S\ref{sec:limitations}), so absolute rates carry more uncertainty than for other types; (2) incompleteness is a \emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\%, 16 pp range); (3) conditional precedence is the most \emph{model-invariant type} (34--40\%, 6 pp range). The authorization scope ranking (Claude $<$ Qwen3 $<$ GPT-4.1) is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap ranking preservation: 99.5\%, $B = 10{,}000$; pairwise orderings $\geq$99.5\%), controlling for judge identity. The 34\,pp within-judge range (GPT-5.4-judged subset) far exceeds measurement noise, but given the low cross-judge $\kappa$, we characterize this pattern as suggestive rather than definitive. Both judges belong to the OpenAI model family; cross-family evaluation (e.g., Claude as judge) would provide independent confirmation. (The low $\kappa$ partly reflects the prevalence paradox: prevalence-adjusted $\kappa$ would be substantially higher; cf.\ the analogous gap in failure-mode coding, \S\ref{sec:limitations}.) Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}), confirming that the C4 interaction concentrates in this single type.
